  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="Agentic RAG → Agentic LLM Wiki

Quick follow-up on this.

I had already built an early version of LLM Wiki Studio around this idea: uploaded sources are compiled into a persistent wiki with source summaries, topic pages, saved answers, index/log pages, and maintenance checks.

I’ve now started extending this into an “Agentic LLM Wiki” system.

Agentic RAG can make retrieval more dynamic: it can plan, search, re-rank, call tools, and inspect more context before answering. But it can also spend a lot of tokens at query time, repeatedly searching through raw sources and re-synthesizing knowledge that the system may have already seen before.

The motivation behind Agentic LLM Wiki is different.

Instead of making every query more retrieval-heavy, the system tries to use the persistent wiki as the first memory layer. When a question comes in, it checks whether the wiki is sufficient. If it is, it answers from the wiki. If not, it falls back to the original sources, answers with the additional context, and then suggests wiki updates so the missing knowledge can become part of the memory.

The goal is to shift work from repeated query-time retrieval to accumulated knowledge maintenance.

In short:

Agentic RAG = smarter search over raw context at query time
Agentic LLM Wiki = persistent synthesis first, raw retrieval only when needed

This could reduce token cost while improving answer quality over time, because useful synthesis is not thrown away after each answer.

Current LLM Wiki Studio:
https://github.com/cagataysengor/llm-wiki-studio

Draft PR for the new Agentic LLM Wiki mode:
https://github.com/cagataysengor/llm-wiki-studio/pull/1
" data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">
      
                Copy Markdown

</clipboard-copy>  <div aria-live="polite" aria-atomic="true" class="sr-only" data-clipboard-copy-feedback></div>
</span>        </details-menu>

</span>              <span data-view-component="true">
  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="## dropped v0.1 of Kompl in this thread a while back — just shipped v0.2, so adding the update.
Repo: https://github.com/tuirk/Kompl


short version for new readers: Kompl runs the pattern from this gist with synthesis at ingest time, not query time. you save a thing (a link, a PDF, a YouTube video, a bookmark export, pasted text) and Kompl reads it as it arrives: pulls out the people, ideas, and arguments inside, writes them into wiki pages that link to each other, and updates existing pages when new sources contribute. save your tenth source on a topic and the page already reflects the pattern across all ten without you having to ask. the wiki itself is the cached synthesis. self-hosted via docker, bring-your-own API keys, MCP server included so an agent can query the compiled wiki.

&lt;img width=&quot;700&quot; alt=&quot;Karpathy&#39;s LLM wiki vs Kompl&quot; src=&quot;https://gist.github.com/user-attachments/assets/f1f22664-66cb-4d23-8c6b-5d7194b21fa1&quot; /&gt;

&lt;br&gt;

### what&#39;s new in v0.2

- multi-provider. DeepSeek V4 Pro added as a second compile backend, selectable per session. Gemini 2.5 has a structured-output truncation pathology on dense inputs (~50K+ char academic PDFs); DeepSeek handles up to ~200K cleanly. provider abstraction layer routes gemini-* and deepseek-* IDs through one LLMProvider interface. per-session model lock stamps the choice at session start so mid-flight settings changes don&#39;t hot-swap.
- live progress UI. per-step X/Y counters during compile (extract, draft, ingest, match, crossref, commit), expand-to-reveal item drill-down, time-estimate shown as a range instead of a single conservative value.
- stranded-source recovery. a source whose extract fails mid-session is no longer unrecoverable. orchestrator re-plans on retry; commit-activation gate only marks compile_status=&#39;active&#39; for sources with an extractions row, so retry routes can re-attempt the source.
- new connectors. paste-text (raw text → source, no URL or file needed). YouTube direct-ingest via the official transcript API + Data API videos.list — replaces the prior silent fallback to scraping watch-page chrome on transcript-less videos. covers watch / youtu.be / shorts / embed / m. / music. URL forms.
- one-line installers. install.sh for macOS/Linux/WSL, install.ps1 for Windows. pre-flights Docker, Node 24, disk, RAM before handing off to the API-key prompts.
- security pass. SSRF hardening on /metadata/peek (DNS-resolved IP pinning, cloud-metadata blocklist, scheme allowlist, manual redirect revalidation), path-traversal containment across nlp-service and Next.js, YAML frontmatter escaping with C0/C1/U+2028/U+2029/BOM stripping, log-arg scrubbing, Scorecard-flagged deps pinned, nlp-service bound to 127.0.0.1.


### **What goes in Kompl:**

- URLs (web pages, articles, YouTube videos, GitHub repos, anything Firecrawl can reach)
- Files (PDF, DOCX, PPTX, XLSX, TXT, MD, HTML, CSV, images, audio)
- Browser bookmark, Twitter/X bookmark, Apple Notes/Upnote exports

Here&#39;s what that looks like after a few sessions; new overviews, comparisons, entity pages, contradictions surfacing, fresh cross-links between everything.
&lt;img src=&quot;https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tuirk/Kompl/main/docs/assets/kompl-demo.gif&quot; width=&quot;700&quot; alt=&quot;Kompl demo&quot; /&gt;

### **A few specific bets we made on top of the pattern:**

- **NLP before LLM.** spaCy NER + a 4-way keyphrase fanout (RAKE, KeyBERT, TextRank, YAKE) runs first; Gemini gets pre-resolved entities, not raw markdown. Cheaper and less noisy.
- **Batch ingest, async compile.** Drop sources, close the tab, come back to a wiki. Server-side pipeline with rate limits, a customizable daily USD cap, and other settings (entity promotion threshold, draft length floor, model tier per session, schema-driven tone — more in the repo).
- **Three layers of entity resolution** (fuzzy, embedding, LLM disambiguation) collapse variations like &quot;GPT 4&quot;, &quot;GPT-4&quot;, and &quot;gpt4&quot; into one canonical.
- **Comparison pages** surface when sources disagree across three or more sources.
- **Wikilinks** get injected deterministically by regex, not by an LLM.
- **MCP-native.** Stdio MCP server (`search_wiki`, `read_page`, `list_pages`, `wiki_stats`) so Claude Code, Claude Desktop, Cursor can use the wiki as a knowledge source out of the box. That&#39;s our favorite feature.
- **For UI** the gist mentions Obsidian as the IDE. Kompl runs in its own UI but ships an Obsidian-compatible export, so you&#39;re not locked in either way.
- **Local Docker, single-tenant**, BYO Gemini + Firecrawl keys. Open-sourced with Apache-2.0.


40-second demo is below, click to watch on Youtube and full details on GitHub: https://github.com/tuirk/Kompl 


[![Watch the demo](https://img.youtube.com/vi/OjLRpnpeFYY/maxresdefault.jpg)](https://youtu.be/OjLRpnpeFYY)

 Fork it, run it on your own sources, let me know how it goes 🥸

Repo: https://github.com/tuirk/Kompl" data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">
      
                Copy Markdown

</clipboard-copy>  <div aria-live="polite" aria-atomic="true" class="sr-only" data-clipboard-copy-feedback></div>
</span>        </details-menu>

<li>one-line installers. install.sh for macOS/Linux/WSL, install.ps1 for Windows. pre-flights Docker, Node 24, disk, RAM before handing off to the API-key prompts.</li>
<li>security pass. SSRF hardening on /metadata/peek (DNS-resolved IP pinning, cloud-metadata blocklist, scheme allowlist, manual redirect revalidation), path-traversal containment across nlp-service and Next.js, YAML frontmatter escaping with C0/C1/U+2028/U+2029/BOM stripping, log-arg scrubbing, Scorecard-flagged deps pinned, nlp-service bound to 127.0.0.1.</li>
</ul>
<h3 dir="auto"><strong>What goes in Kompl:</strong></h3>
<ul dir="auto">
<li>URLs (web pages, articles, YouTube videos, GitHub repos, anything Firecrawl can reach)</li>
<li>Files (PDF, DOCX, PPTX, XLSX, TXT, MD, HTML, CSV, images, audio)</li>
<li>Browser bookmark, Twitter/X bookmark, Apple Notes/Upnote exports</li>
</ul>
<p dir="auto">Here's what that looks like after a few sessions; new overviews, comparisons, entity pages, contradictions surfacing, fresh cross-links between everything.<br>
<a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tuirk/Kompl/main/docs/assets/kompl-demo.gif"><img src="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tuirk/Kompl/main/docs/assets/kompl-demo.gif" width="700" alt="Kompl demo" data-animated-image="" style="max-width: 100%;"></a></p>
<h3 dir="auto"><strong>A few specific bets we made on top of the pattern:</strong></h3>
<ul dir="auto">
<li><strong>NLP before LLM.</strong> spaCy NER + a 4-way keyphrase fanout (RAKE, KeyBERT, TextRank, YAKE) runs first; Gemini gets pre-resolved entities, not raw markdown. Cheaper and less noisy.</li>
<li><strong>Batch ingest, async compile.</strong> Drop sources, close the tab, come back to a wiki. Server-side pipeline with rate limits, a customizable daily USD cap, and other settings (entity promotion threshold, draft length floor, model tier per session, schema-driven tone — more in the repo).</li>
<li><strong>Three layers of entity resolution</strong> (fuzzy, embedding, LLM disambiguation) collapse variations like "GPT 4", "GPT-4", and "gpt4" into one canonical.</li>
<li><strong>Comparison pages</strong> surface when sources disagree across three or more sources.</li>
<li><strong>Wikilinks</strong> get injected deterministically by regex, not by an LLM.</li>
<li><strong>MCP-native.</strong> Stdio MCP server (<code class="notranslate">search_wiki</code>, <code class="notranslate">read_page</code>, <code class="notranslate">list_pages</code>, <code class="notranslate">wiki_stats</code>) so Claude Code, Claude Desktop, Cursor can use the wiki as a knowledge source out of the box. That's our favorite feature.</li>
<li><strong>For UI</strong> the gist mentions Obsidian as the IDE. Kompl runs in its own UI but ships an Obsidian-compatible export, so you're not locked in either way.</li>
<li><strong>Local Docker, single-tenant</strong>, BYO Gemini + Firecrawl keys. Open-sourced with Apache-2.0.</li>
</ul>
<p dir="auto">40-second demo is below, click to watch on Youtube and full details on GitHub: <a href="https://github.com/tuirk/Kompl">https://github.com/tuirk/Kompl</a></p>
<p dir="auto"><a href="https://youtu.be/OjLRpnpeFYY" rel="nofollow"><img src="https://camo.githubusercontent.com/f3b2ec75f6bef805a8e0f8b3e1bec8f8867619d22d5a99a6426423da159bcc0d/68747470733a2f2f696d672e796f75747562652e636f6d2f76692f4f6a4c52706e70654659592f6d617872657364656661756c742e6a7067" alt="Watch the demo" data-canonical-src="https://img.youtube.com/vi/OjLRpnpeFYY/maxresdefault.jpg" style="max-width: 100%;"></a></p>
<p dir="auto">Fork it, run it on your own sources, let me know how it goes 🥸</p>
<p dir="auto">Repo: <a href="https://github.com/tuirk/Kompl">https://github.com/tuirk/Kompl</a></p>
    </div>
  </task-lists>
  
</div>

      <p dir="auto">Thanks for sharing this, <a class="user-mention notranslate" data-hovercard-type="user" data-hovercard-url="/users/karpathy/hovercard" data-octo-click="hovercard-link-click" data-octo-dimensions="link_type:self" href="https://github.com/karpathy">@karpathy</a> — really insightful.</p>

  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="AKBP turns the LLM Wiki pattern into a protocol surface for agent runtimes. It is a local-first, file-backed knowledge base that agents can read, write, verify, export, and carry across tools.

The idea comes from the same insight behind [LLM Wiki v2](https://gist.github.com/rohitg00/2067ab416f7bbe447c1977edaaa681e2): stop re-deriving, start compiling. AKBP adds the machinery a repo needs when that pattern becomes operational: typed claims, source hashes, lifecycle relations, review-gated writes, JSONL tool calls, schemas, and conformance tests.

This repository contains the reference implementation:

a Python CLI for creating and maintaining AKBP knowledge bases
a newline-delimited JSON tool server for agent integrations
JSON schemas for requests, responses, records, and method parameters
adapter templates for coding-agent runtimes
conformance checks, benchmark fixtures, import/export checks, and CI validation

https://github.com/rohitg00/akbp" data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">
      
                Copy Markdown

</clipboard-copy>  <div aria-live="polite" aria-atomic="true" class="sr-only" data-clipboard-copy-feedback></div>
</span>        </details-menu>
      </details>

  <h3 class="f5 text-normal py-2" style="flex: 1 1 auto">
    <div class="d-flex flex-items-center flex-wrap gap-1">
      <span class="d-inline-flex">
          
      </span>

      <strong>
            <a class="author Link--primary text-bold css-overflow-wrap-anywhere " show_full_name="false" data-hovercard-type="user" data-hovercard-url="/users/rohitg00/hovercard" data-octo-click="hovercard-link-click" data-octo-dimensions="link_type:self" href="/rohitg00">rohitg00</a>
  

      </strong>

      

      commented 


        <a href="/karpathy/442a6bf555914893e9891c11519de94f?permalink_comment_id=6144497#gistcomment-6144497" id="gistcomment-6144497-permalink" class="Link--secondary js-timestamp"><relative-time datetime="2026-05-11T15:50:07Z" class="no-wrap">May 11, 2026</relative-time></a>


      
    </div>

  </h3>
</div>


        <div class="edit-comment-hide">
  <task-lists disabled sortable>
    <div class="comment-body markdown-body js-comment-body soft-wrap user-select-contain d-block">
      <p dir="auto">AKBP turns the LLM Wiki pattern into a protocol surface for agent runtimes. It is a local-first, file-backed knowledge base that agents can read, write, verify, export, and carry across tools.</p>
<p dir="auto">The idea comes from the same insight behind <a href="https://gist.github.com/rohitg00/2067ab416f7bbe447c1977edaaa681e2">LLM Wiki v2</a>: stop re-deriving, start compiling. AKBP adds the machinery a repo needs when that pattern becomes operational: typed claims, source hashes, lifecycle relations, review-gated writes, JSONL tool calls, schemas, and conformance tests.</p>
<p dir="auto">This repository contains the reference implementation:</p>
<p dir="auto">a Python CLI for creating and maintaining AKBP knowledge bases<br>
a newline-delimited JSON tool server for agent integrations<br>
JSON schemas for requests, responses, records, and method parameters<br>
adapter templates for coding-agent runtimes<br>
conformance checks, benchmark fixtures, import/export checks, and CI validation</p>
<p dir="auto"><a href="https://github.com/rohitg00/akbp">https://github.com/rohitg00/akbp</a></p>
    </div>
  </task-lists>
  
</div>

    <div id="gistcomment-6145955" class="timeline-comment-group js-minimizable-comment-group js-targetable-element my-0 comment previewable-edit js-task-list-container js-comment timeline-comment timeline-comment--caret tmp-ml-n3 js-minimize-container unminimized-comment">

</span>              <span data-view-component="true">
  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="**LLM Wiki — A Knowledge Management Revolution or a Transactional Dead End?**

The &quot;LLM Wiki&quot; concept (recently popularized following Andrej Karpathy&#39;s proposal) looks like a silver bullet for personal and corporate knowledge management. The premise is elegant: an AI transforms a chaotic mess of thousands of files and emails into a structured network of Markdown documents, complete with auto-generated meta-descriptions and cross-links.

However, beneath the initial convenience lies a fundamental architectural challenge that the industry is only beginning to whisper about. When moving from a &quot;10-file demo&quot; to a real-world archive — such as a CEO’s ten-year history of correspondence and documentation — we discover that instead of a simple file system, we are attempting to build a highly complex and expensive DBMS on top of neural networks.

**1. The Illusion of &quot;Easy&quot; Updates (Transactional Overhead)**
Most of the current hype focuses on the Read phase. AI is excellent at summarizing and tagging. But as soon as we move to Write or update operations, the system hits a transactional nightmare:

Any single change in one document can trigger a cascade, requiring the revision of dozens of links in other files.

In a classical SQL database, this is handled by indexes in milliseconds. In an LLM Wiki, this requires a chain of model calls that consume both significant time and tokens.

**2. The Crisis of Link Integrity**
In Karpathy’s approach, the AI assumes the role of a Database Architect. However, AI lacks the inherent concept of Referential Integrity:

If a file is deleted or renamed, hundreds of &quot;smart links&quot; in other .md files instantly become &quot;dead.&quot;

To keep the database up to date, one needs a constant background process to &quot;re-wire&quot; the entire knowledge web. This transforms a simple folder of files into a heavy, ongoing ETL process.

**3. Temporal Degradation in Large Archives**
For a CEO managing a decade-old archive, chronology is critical. Standard vector searches often suffer from &quot;temporal blindness,&quot; resurfacing data from 2018 as if it were current.

Without a rigid metadata structure and &quot;layering&quot; (Hot Data vs. Cold Archive), the system begins to hallucinate contexts, blurring the lines between contract terms from different years.

**4. Summary: Read-Only vs. Active Storage**
The current excitement is justified for static archives — it is arguably the best way to quickly &quot;resurface&quot; the history of old projects. But for a live corporate environment, the &quot;just a folder of Markdown files&quot; approach is a path toward losing control over your data.

**We are on the threshold of a new class of software: AI-Native Databases. These won&#39;t just be folders; they will be hybrids that combine the rigid logic of SQL (for transaction and link control) with the cognitive flexibility of an LLM (for semantic understanding). Without this fundamental layer, a &quot;smart wiki&quot; at scale will inevitably devolve into a digital landfill with polished headers.**" data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">
      
                Copy Markdown

</clipboard-copy>  <div aria-live="polite" aria-atomic="true" class="sr-only" data-clipboard-copy-feedback></div>
</span>        </details-menu>
      </details>
    </div>

    </div>
  </div>

  <h3 class="f5 text-normal py-2" style="flex: 1 1 auto">

      </span>

      </strong>

      

      commented

    </div>

  </h3>

    <div class="comment-body markdown-body js-comment-body soft-wrap user-select-contain d-block">
      <p dir="auto"><strong>LLM Wiki — A Knowledge Management Revolution or a Transactional Dead End?</strong></p>
<p dir="auto">The "LLM Wiki" concept (recently popularized following Andrej Karpathy's proposal) looks like a silver bullet for personal and corporate knowledge management. The premise is elegant: an AI transforms a chaotic mess of thousands of files and emails into a structured network of Markdown documents, complete with auto-generated meta-descriptions and cross-links.</p>
<p dir="auto">However, beneath the initial convenience lies a fundamental architectural challenge that the industry is only beginning to whisper about. When moving from a "10-file demo" to a real-world archive — such as a CEO’s ten-year history of correspondence and documentation — we discover that instead of a simple file system, we are attempting to build a highly complex and expensive DBMS on top of neural networks.</p>
<p dir="auto"><strong>1. The Illusion of "Easy" Updates (Transactional Overhead)</strong><br>
Most of the current hype focuses on the Read phase. AI is excellent at summarizing and tagging. But as soon as we move to Write or update operations, the system hits a transactional nightmare:</p>
<p dir="auto">Any single change in one document can trigger a cascade, requiring the revision of dozens of links in other files.</p>
<p dir="auto">In a classical SQL database, this is handled by indexes in milliseconds. In an LLM Wiki, this requires a chain of model calls that consume both significant time and tokens.</p>
<p dir="auto"><strong>2. The Crisis of Link Integrity</strong><br>
In Karpathy’s approach, the AI assumes the role of a Database Architect. However, AI lacks the inherent concept of Referential Integrity:</p>
<p dir="auto">If a file is deleted or renamed, hundreds of "smart links" in other .md files instantly become "dead."</p>
<p dir="auto">To keep the database up to date, one needs a constant background process to "re-wire" the entire knowledge web. This transforms a simple folder of files into a heavy, ongoing ETL process.</p>
<p dir="auto"><strong>3. Temporal Degradation in Large Archives</strong><br>
For a CEO managing a decade-old archive, chronology is critical. Standard vector searches often suffer from "temporal blindness," resurfacing data from 2018 as if it were current.</p>
<p dir="auto">Without a rigid metadata structure and "layering" (Hot Data vs. Cold Archive), the system begins to hallucinate contexts, blurring the lines between contract terms from different years.</p>
<p dir="auto"><strong>4. Summary: Read-Only vs. Active Storage</strong><br>
The current excitement is justified for static archives — it is arguably the best way to quickly "resurface" the history of old projects. But for a live corporate environment, the "just a folder of Markdown files" approach is a path toward losing control over your data.</p>
<p dir="auto"><strong>We are on the threshold of a new class of software: AI-Native Databases. These won't just be folders; they will be hybrids that combine the rigid logic of SQL (for transaction and link control) with the cognitive flexibility of an LLM (for semantic understanding). Without this fundamental layer, a "smart wiki" at scale will inevitably devolve into a digital landfill with polished headers.</strong></p>
    </div>
  </task-lists>
  
</div>

  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="&lt;p align=&quot;center&quot;&gt;
  &lt;pre&gt;
  ███████╗ ██████╗ ███████╗
  ██╔════╝██╔═══██╗╚══███╔╝
  ███████╗██║   ██║  ███╔╝
  ╚════██║██║▄▄ ██║ ███╔╝
  ███████║╚██████╔╝███████╗
  ╚══════╝ ╚══▀▀═╝ ╚══════╝
  &lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;p align=&quot;center&quot;&gt;
  &lt;strong&gt;Compress LLM context to save tokens and reduce costs&lt;/strong&gt;
&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;p align=&quot;center&quot;&gt;
  &lt;sub&gt;
    &lt;strong&gt;Real session stats:&lt;/strong&gt;
    3,003 compressions ·
    &lt;strong&gt;178,442 tokens saved&lt;/strong&gt; ·
    24.7% avg reduction · up to
    &lt;strong&gt;92%&lt;/strong&gt; with dedup
  &lt;/sub&gt;
&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;p align=&quot;center&quot;&gt;
  &lt;a href=&quot;https://thenextgentechinsider.com/pulse/sqz-tool-cuts-llm-token-use-by-92-for-file-heavy-ai-tasks&quot;&gt;&lt;img src=&quot;https://img.shields.io/badge/%231_Featured-NextGen_Tech_Insider-ff6600?style=for-the-badge&amp;logo=newspaper&amp;logoColor=white&quot; alt=&quot;Featured&quot;&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;p align=&quot;center&quot;&gt;
  &lt;a href=&quot;https://crates.io/crates/sqz-cli&quot;&gt;&lt;img src=&quot;https://img.shields.io/crates/v/sqz-cli?logo=rust&amp;logoColor=white&amp;label=crates.io&amp;color=e6522c&quot; alt=&quot;Crates.io&quot;&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
  &lt;a href=&quot;https://www.npmjs.com/package/sqz-cli&quot;&gt;&lt;img src=&quot;https://img.shields.io/npm/v/sqz-cli?logo=npm&amp;logoColor=white&amp;label=npm&amp;color=cb3837&quot; alt=&quot;npm&quot;&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
  &lt;a href=&quot;https://pypi.org/project/sqz/&quot;&gt;&lt;img src=&quot;https://img.shields.io/pypi/v/sqz?logo=python&amp;logoColor=white&amp;label=PyPI&amp;color=3775a9&quot; alt=&quot;PyPI&quot;&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
  &lt;a href=&quot;https://marketplace.visualstudio.com/items?itemName=ojuschugh1.sqz&quot;&gt;&lt;img src=&quot;https://img.shields.io/badge/VS%20Code-Marketplace-007acc?logo=visual-studio-code&amp;logoColor=white&quot; alt=&quot;VS Code&quot;&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
  &lt;a href=&quot;https://addons.mozilla.org/en-US/firefox/addon/sqz-context-compression/&quot;&gt;&lt;img src=&quot;https://img.shields.io/badge/Firefox-Add--on-ff7139?logo=firefox-browser&amp;logoColor=white&quot; alt=&quot;Firefox&quot;&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
  &lt;a href=&quot;https://plugins.jetbrains.com/plugin/31240-sqz--context-intelligence/&quot;&gt;&lt;img src=&quot;https://img.shields.io/badge/JetBrains-Plugin-000000?logo=jetbrains&amp;logoColor=white&quot; alt=&quot;JetBrains&quot;&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
  &lt;a href=&quot;https://discord.gg/j8EEyH5dSB&quot;&gt;&lt;img src=&quot;https://img.shields.io/discord/1493251029075235076?logo=discord&amp;logoColor=white&amp;label=Discord&amp;color=5865F2&quot; alt=&quot;Discord&quot;&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
  &lt;a href=&quot;https://github.com/ojuschugh1/homebrew-sqz&quot;&gt;&lt;img src=&quot;https://img.shields.io/badge/Homebrew-tap-FBB040?logo=homebrew&amp;logoColor=white&quot; alt=&quot;Homebrew&quot;&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;p align=&quot;center&quot;&gt;
  &lt;a href=&quot;#install&quot;&gt;Install&lt;/a&gt; ·
  &lt;a href=&quot;#how-it-works&quot;&gt;How It Works&lt;/a&gt; ·
  &lt;a href=&quot;#supported-tools&quot;&gt;Supported Tools&lt;/a&gt; ·
  &lt;a href=&quot;CHANGELOG.md&quot;&gt;Changelog&lt;/a&gt; ·
  &lt;a href=&quot;https://discord.gg/j8EEyH5dSB&quot;&gt;Discord&lt;/a&gt;
&lt;/p&gt;

---

sqz compresses command output before it reaches your LLM. Single Rust binary, zero config.

The real win is dedup: when the same file gets read 5 times in a session, sqz sends it once and returns a 13-token reference for every repeat.

```
Without sqz:                    With sqz:

File read #1:  2,000 tokens     File read #1:  ~800 tokens (compressed)
File read #2:  2,000 tokens     File read #2:  ~13 tokens  (dedup ref)
File read #3:  2,000 tokens     File read #3:  ~13 tokens  (dedup ref)
───────────────────────         ───────────────────────
Total:         6,000 tokens     Total:         ~826 tokens (86% saved)
```

## Token Savings

&gt; **24.7%** average reduction across 3,003 real compressions ·
&gt; **92%** saved on repeated file reads ·
&gt; **86%** on shell/git output ·
&gt; **13-token** refs for cached content

One developer&#39;s week, measured from actual `sqz gain` output:

```
$ sqz gain
sqz token savings (last 7 days)
──────────────────────────────────────────────────
  04-13 │                              │   2,329 saved
  04-14 │                              │       0 saved
  04-15 │███                           │  12,954 saved
  04-16 │██                            │   9,223 saved
  04-17 │████                          │  14,752 saved
  04-18 │██████████████████████████████│ 105,569 saved
  04-19 │████████                      │  30,882 saved
  04-20 │█                             │   4,334 saved
──────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Total: 3,003 compressions, 178,442 tokens saved (24.7% avg reduction)
```

### Per-command compression

Single-command compression (measured via `cargo test -p sqz-engine benchmarks`):

| Content | Before | After | Saved |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Repeated log lines | 148 | 62 | **58%** |
| Large JSON array | 259 | 142 | **45%** |
| JSON API response | 64 | 53 | **17%** |
| Git diff | 61 | 54 | **12%** |
| Prose/docs | 124 | 121 | **2%** |
| Stack trace (safe mode) | 82 | 82 | **0%** |

### Session-level with dedup

Where the real savings live — the cache sends each file once, repeats cost 13 tokens:

| Scenario | Without sqz | With sqz | Saved |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Same file read 5× | 10,000 | 826 | **92%** |
| Same JSON response 3× | 192 | 79 | **59%** |
| Test-fix-test cycle (3 runs) | 15,000 | 5,186 | **65%** |

Single-command compression ranges from 2–58% depending on content. Repeated reads drop to 13 tokens each. Your mileage will vary with how repetitive your tool calls are — agentic sessions with many file re-reads see the biggest wins.

## Install

**Prebuilt binaries** (no compiler required — works on every platform):

```sh
# macOS / Linux
curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ojuschugh1/sqz/main/install.sh | sh

# Windows (PowerShell)
irm https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ojuschugh1/sqz/main/install.ps1 | iex

# Any platform via npm
npm install -g sqz-cli

# macOS / Linux via Homebrew
brew tap ojuschugh1/sqz
brew install sqz
```

**Build from source via Cargo:**

```sh
cargo install sqz-cli sqz-mcp
```

`sqz-cli` provides the `sqz` binary; `sqz-mcp` provides the MCP server. `sqz-engine` is a library dependency — it compiles automatically and does not need to be installed separately.

**Build from source** (`cargo install sqz-cli`) works too, but needs a C toolchain:

- Linux: `build-essential` (apt) or equivalent
- macOS: Xcode Command Line Tools (`xcode-select --install`)
- **Windows: Visual Studio Build Tools with the &quot;Desktop development with C++&quot; workload.** Without these, `cargo install` fails with `linker link.exe not found`. If you don&#39;t already have them, use the PowerShell or npm install above instead.

Then initialize:

```sh
sqz init --global     # hooks apply to every project on this machine
# or
sqz init              # hooks apply to just this project (.claude/settings.local.json)
```

`--global` writes to `~/.claude/settings.json` (the user scope per the
[Anthropic scope table](https://docs.claude.com/en/docs/claude-code/settings)),
so the sqz hook fires in every Claude Code session on this machine. This is
the common case on first install. Your existing `permissions`, `env`,
`statusLine`, and unrelated hooks in `~/.claude/settings.json` are
preserved — sqz merges its entries rather than overwriting.

Plain `sqz init` (project scope) is useful when you want sqz active only
inside one repo.

**Only using one agent?** Pass `--only` (or `--skip`) to limit which
configs are written:

```sh
sqz init --only opencode              # just OpenCode, nothing else
sqz init --only opencode,codex        # OpenCode and Codex
sqz init --skip cursor,windsurf       # everything except Cursor and Windsurf
```

Accepted names: `claude`, `cursor`, `windsurf`, `cline`, `gemini`,
`kiro`, `opencode`, `codex`. Aliases (`claude-code`, `gemini-cli`, `roo`,
`kiro-cli`) also work. `--only` and `--skip` can&#39;t be combined.

### Manual installation (preserve comments in your config)

`sqz init` round-trips your config file through a JSON parser to merge
the sqz entry, which drops any comments in your `opencode.jsonc` (and
the analogous JSON-with-comments files other tools accept). If you&#39;ve
commented your config carefully and want to keep them, install by hand
instead.

**OpenCode** — two steps:

1. Drop the plugin file in place. `sqz` prints the generated TS to
   stdout so you don&#39;t have to hand-write the path-escaping logic:

   ```sh
   mkdir -p ~/.config/opencode/plugins
   sqz print-opencode-plugin &gt; ~/.config/opencode/plugins/sqz.ts
   ```

2. Add the MCP entry to your existing `opencode.jsonc` yourself.
   Append this block inside the top-level `mcp` object (create the
   `mcp` object if it doesn&#39;t exist):

   ```jsonc
   &quot;sqz&quot;: {
     &quot;type&quot;: &quot;local&quot;,
     &quot;command&quot;: [&quot;sqz-mcp&quot;, &quot;--transport&quot;, &quot;stdio&quot;],
     &quot;enabled&quot;: true
   }
   ```

Comments in the rest of your file stay put. OpenCode auto-discovers
the plugin file; no `plugin` array entry needed (adding one causes
double-loading, see issue #10).

**Other tools** — Claude Code, Cursor, Windsurf, Cline, Gemini CLI,
and Codex use plain JSON configs without comment support, so the
automated path is non-destructive there. Use `sqz init --only &lt;tool&gt;`
for those.

That&#39;s it. Shell hooks installed, AI tool hooks configured.

## How It Works

&lt;p align=&quot;center&quot;&gt;
  &lt;img src=&quot;assets/sqz-architecture.png&quot; alt=&quot;sqz system architecture&quot; width=&quot;700&quot; /&gt;
&lt;/p&gt;

sqz installs a PreToolUse hook that intercepts bash commands before your AI tool runs them. The output gets compressed transparently — the AI tool never knows.

```
Claude → git status → [sqz hook rewrites] → compressed output (85% smaller)
```

What gets compressed:
- **Shell output** — git, cargo, npm, docker, kubectl, ls, grep, etc.
- **JSON** — strips nulls, compact encoding
- **Logs** — collapses repeated lines
- **Test output** — shows failures only

What doesn&#39;t get compressed:
- Stack traces, error messages, secrets — routed to safe mode (0% compression)
- Your prompts and the AI&#39;s responses — controlled by the AI tool, not sqz

## Supported Tools

| Tool | Integration | Setup |
|---|---|---|
| Claude Code | PreToolUse hook (transparent) | `sqz init` |
| Cursor | PreToolUse hook (transparent) | `sqz init` |
| Windsurf | PreToolUse hook (transparent) | `sqz init` |
| Cline | PreToolUse hook (transparent) | `sqz init` |
| Gemini CLI | BeforeTool hook (transparent) | `sqz init` |
| Kiro | PreToolUse hook (transparent) | `sqz init` |
| OpenCode | TypeScript plugin (transparent) | `sqz init` |
| VS Code | [Extension](https://marketplace.visualstudio.com/items?itemName=ojuschugh1.sqz) | Install from Marketplace |
| JetBrains | [Plugin](https://plugins.jetbrains.com/plugin/31240-sqz--context-intelligence/) | Install from Marketplace |
| Chrome | Browser extension | ChatGPT, Claude.ai, Gemini, Grok, Perplexity |
| [Firefox](https://addons.mozilla.org/en-US/firefox/addon/sqz-context-compression/) | Browser extension | Same sites |

## CLI

```sh
sqz init --global             # Install hooks for every project on this machine
sqz init                      # Install hooks for just this project
sqz init --only kiro          # Only configure Kiro (skip the rest)
sqz init --only opencode      # Only configure OpenCode (skip the rest)
sqz init --skip cursor        # Configure every agent except Cursor
sqz compress &lt;text&gt;           # Compress (or pipe from stdin)
sqz compress --no-cache       # Compress without dedup (always full output)
sqz expand &lt;ref&gt;              # Recover original content from a §ref:HASH§ token
sqz compact                   # Evict stale context to free tokens
sqz gain                      # Show daily token savings (bar chart)
sqz gain --project .          # Per-project daily gains
sqz gain --days 30            # Last 30 days
sqz stats                     # Cumulative compression report
sqz stats --breakdown         # Per-command token usage breakdown
sqz stats --project .         # Stats for current project only
sqz stats --project list      # List all tracked projects
sqz discover                  # Find missed savings
sqz resume                    # Re-inject session context after compaction
sqz vizit                     # Live terminal dashboard (like htop for AI agents)
sqz hook claude               # Process a PreToolUse hook (Claude Code)
sqz hook kiro                 # Process a PreToolUse hook (Kiro)
sqz print-opencode-plugin     # Print OpenCode plugin TS for manual install
sqz proxy --port 8080         # API proxy (compresses full request payloads)
```

### Dedup Escape Hatch

When sqz sees the same content twice, it returns a compact `§ref:HASH§` token
instead of the full text. Most models handle this fine, but some (e.g., GLM 5.1)
can&#39;t parse the ref format and loop. Four ways to work around this:

```sh
# 1. Recover original content from a ref
sqz expand a1b2c3d4              # prefix match
sqz expand &#39;§ref:a1b2c3d4§&#39;     # paste the whole token

# 2. Compress without dedup (per-invocation)
echo &quot;...&quot; | sqz compress --no-cache

# 3. Disable dedup globally (env var)
export SQZ_NO_DEDUP=1

# 4. MCP passthrough tool (returns input byte-exact, zero transforms)
# Available via tools/list when sqz-mcp is running
```

## Track Your Own Savings

Run `sqz gain` in your shell any time to see your own daily breakdown (see the
Token Savings section above for what the output looks like), and `sqz stats`
for the full cumulative report:

```sh
$ sqz stats
  📊 sqz compression stats
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────

  178,442  tokens saved
  ↓  24.7% average reduction

  Compressions           3,003
  Tokens in              721,840
  Tokens out             543,398
  Tokens saved           178,442
  Avg reduction          24.7%

  🗄️  Cache
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Entries                43
  Size                   39.1 KB
```

Add `--breakdown` to see exactly which commands consume the most tokens:

```sh
$ sqz stats --breakdown

  🔍 Top Token Consumers
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  command               calls  tokens in        out    saved
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  dedup                   249      45541       3237      93%
  stdin                    51      30851      24289      21%
  auto                    132      18288       7740      58%
  echo                     17       1050        558      47%
  ls -la                    8        948        948       0%
  cargo build               7        170        145      15%
  git status                4         56          8      86%
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
```

**Per-project filtering:**

```sh
sqz stats --project .           # stats for current project only
sqz stats --project list        # list all tracked projects
sqz gain --project .            # daily gains for current project
sqz gain --days 30              # last 30 days instead of 7
sqz gain --days 30 --project .  # combine both
```

Stats are stored locally in SQLite under `~/.sqz/sessions.db` — nothing leaves your machine.

## How Compression Works

1. **Per-command formatters** — `git status` → compact summary, `cargo test` → failures only, `docker ps` → name/image/status table
2. **Structural summaries** — code files compressed to imports + function signatures + call graph (~70% reduction). The model sees the architecture, not implementation noise.
3. **Dedup cache** — SHA-256 content hash, persistent across sessions. Second read = 13-token reference.
4. **JSON pipeline** — strip nulls → project out debug fields → flatten → collapse arrays → TOON encoding (lossless compact format)
5. **Safe mode** — stack traces, secrets, migrations detected by entropy analysis and routed through with 0% compression

For the full technical details, see [docs/](docs/).

## Configuration

```toml
# ~/.sqz/presets/default.toml
[preset]
name = &quot;default&quot;
version = &quot;1.0&quot;

[compression.condense]
enabled = true
max_repeated_lines = 3

[compression.strip_nulls]
enabled = true

[budget]
warning_threshold = 0.70
default_window_size = 200000
```

## Privacy

- Zero telemetry — no data transmitted, no crash reports
- Fully offline — works in air-gapped environments
- All processing local

## Development

```sh
git clone https://github.com/ojuschugh1/sqz.git
cd sqz
cargo test --workspace
cargo build --release
```

<hr>
<p dir="auto">sqz compresses command output before it reaches your LLM. Single Rust binary, zero config.</p>
<p dir="auto">The real win is dedup: when the same file gets read 5 times in a session, sqz sends it once and returns a 13-token reference for every repeat.</p>
<pre class="notranslate"><code class="notranslate">Without sqz:                    With sqz:

File read #1:  2,000 tokens     File read #1:  ~800 tokens (compressed)
File read #2:  2,000 tokens     File read #2:  ~13 tokens  (dedup ref)
File read #3:  2,000 tokens     File read #3:  ~13 tokens  (dedup ref)
───────────────────────         ───────────────────────
Total:         6,000 tokens     Total:         ~826 tokens (86% saved)
</code></pre>
<h2 dir="auto">Token Savings</h2>
<blockquote>
<p dir="auto"><strong>24.7%</strong> average reduction across 3,003 real compressions ·<br>
<strong>92%</strong> saved on repeated file reads ·<br>
<strong>86%</strong> on shell/git output ·<br>
<strong>13-token</strong> refs for cached content</p>
</blockquote>
<p dir="auto">One developer's week, measured from actual <code class="notranslate">sqz gain</code> output:</p>
<pre class="notranslate"><code class="notranslate">$ sqz gain
sqz token savings (last 7 days)
──────────────────────────────────────────────────
  04-13 │                              │   2,329 saved
  04-14 │                              │       0 saved
  04-15 │███                           │  12,954 saved
  04-16 │██                            │   9,223 saved
  04-17 │████                          │  14,752 saved
  04-18 │██████████████████████████████│ 105,569 saved
  04-19 │████████                      │  30,882 saved
  04-20 │█                             │   4,334 saved
──────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Total: 3,003 compressions, 178,442 tokens saved (24.7% avg reduction)
</code></pre>

<h3 dir="auto">Session-level with dedup</h3>
<p dir="auto">Where the real savings live — the cache sends each file once, repeats cost 13 tokens:</p>
<markdown-accessiblity-table><table role="table">
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Scenario</th>
<th align="right">Without sqz</th>
<th align="right">With sqz</th>

<p dir="auto">Single-command compression ranges from 2–58% depending on content. Repeated reads drop to 13 tokens each. Your mileage will vary with how repetitive your tool calls are — agentic sessions with many file re-reads see the biggest wins.</p>
<h2 dir="auto">Install</h2>
<p dir="auto"><strong>Prebuilt binaries</strong> (no compiler required — works on every platform):</p>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-shell" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate"><span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> macOS / Linux</span>
curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ojuschugh1/sqz/main/install.sh <span class="pl-k">|</span> sh

<span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Windows (PowerShell)</span>
irm https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ojuschugh1/sqz/main/install.ps1 <span class="pl-k">|</span> iex

<span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Any platform via npm</span>
npm install -g sqz-cli

<span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> macOS / Linux via Homebrew</span>
brew tap ojuschugh1/sqz
brew install sqz</pre></div>
<p dir="auto"><strong>Build from source via Cargo:</strong></p>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-shell" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate">cargo install sqz-cli sqz-mcp</pre></div>
<p dir="auto"><code class="notranslate">sqz-cli</code> provides the <code class="notranslate">sqz</code> binary; <code class="notranslate">sqz-mcp</code> provides the MCP server. <code class="notranslate">sqz-engine</code> is a library dependency — it compiles automatically and does not need to be installed separately.</p>
<p dir="auto"><strong>Build from source</strong> (<code class="notranslate">cargo install sqz-cli</code>) works too, but needs a C toolchain:</p>
<ul dir="auto">
<li>Linux: <code class="notranslate">build-essential</code> (apt) or equivalent</li>
<li>macOS: Xcode Command Line Tools (<code class="notranslate">xcode-select --install</code>)</li>
<li><strong>Windows: Visual Studio Build Tools with the "Desktop development with C++" workload.</strong> Without these, <code class="notranslate">cargo install</code> fails with <code class="notranslate">linker link.exe not found</code>. If you don't already have them, use the PowerShell or npm install above instead.</li>
</ul>
<p dir="auto">Then initialize:</p>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-shell" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate">sqz init --global     <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> hooks apply to every project on this machine</span>
<span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> or</span>
sqz init              <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> hooks apply to just this project (.claude/settings.local.json)</span></pre></div>
<p dir="auto"><code class="notranslate">--global</code> writes to <code class="notranslate">~/.claude/settings.json</code> (the user scope per the<br>
<a href="https://docs.claude.com/en/docs/claude-code/settings" rel="nofollow">Anthropic scope table</a>),<br>
so the sqz hook fires in every Claude Code session on this machine. This is<br>
the common case on first install. Your existing <code class="notranslate">permissions</code>, <code class="notranslate">env</code>,<br>
<code class="notranslate">statusLine</code>, and unrelated hooks in <code class="notranslate">~/.claude/settings.json</code> are<br>
preserved — sqz merges its entries rather than overwriting.</p>
<p dir="auto">Plain <code class="notranslate">sqz init</code> (project scope) is useful when you want sqz active only<br>
inside one repo.</p>
<p dir="auto"><strong>Only using one agent?</strong> Pass <code class="notranslate">--only</code> (or <code class="notranslate">--skip</code>) to limit which<br>
configs are written:</p>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-shell" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate">sqz init --only opencode              <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> just OpenCode, nothing else</span>
sqz init --only opencode,codex        <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> OpenCode and Codex</span>
sqz init --skip cursor,windsurf       <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> everything except Cursor and Windsurf</span></pre></div>
<p dir="auto">Accepted names: <code class="notranslate">claude</code>, <code class="notranslate">cursor</code>, <code class="notranslate">windsurf</code>, <code class="notranslate">cline</code>, <code class="notranslate">gemini</code>,<br>
<code class="notranslate">kiro</code>, <code class="notranslate">opencode</code>, <code class="notranslate">codex</code>. Aliases (<code class="notranslate">claude-code</code>, <code class="notranslate">gemini-cli</code>, <code class="notranslate">roo</code>,<br>
<code class="notranslate">kiro-cli</code>) also work. <code class="notranslate">--only</code> and <code class="notranslate">--skip</code> can't be combined.</p>
<h3 dir="auto">Manual installation (preserve comments in your config)</h3>
<p dir="auto"><code class="notranslate">sqz init</code> round-trips your config file through a JSON parser to merge<br>
the sqz entry, which drops any comments in your <code class="notranslate">opencode.jsonc</code> (and<br>
the analogous JSON-with-comments files other tools accept). If you've<br>
commented your config carefully and want to keep them, install by hand<br>
instead.</p>
<p dir="auto"><strong>OpenCode</strong> — two steps:</p>
<ol dir="auto">
<li>
<p dir="auto">Drop the plugin file in place. <code class="notranslate">sqz</code> prints the generated TS to<br>
stdout so you don't have to hand-write the path-escaping logic:</p>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-shell" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate">mkdir -p <span class="pl-k">~</span>/.config/opencode/plugins
sqz print-opencode-plugin <span class="pl-k">&gt;</span> <span class="pl-k">~</span>/.config/opencode/plugins/sqz.ts</pre></div>
</li>
<li>
<p dir="auto">Add the MCP entry to your existing <code class="notranslate">opencode.jsonc</code> yourself.<br>
Append this block inside the top-level <code class="notranslate">mcp</code> object (create the<br>
<code class="notranslate">mcp</code> object if it doesn't exist):</p>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-json-comments" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate"><span class="pl-s"><span class="pl-pds">"</span>sqz<span class="pl-pds">"</span></span>: {
  <span class="pl-ent">"type"</span>: <span class="pl-s"><span class="pl-pds">"</span>local<span class="pl-pds">"</span></span>,
  <span class="pl-ent">"command"</span>: [<span class="pl-s"><span class="pl-pds">"</span>sqz-mcp<span class="pl-pds">"</span></span>, <span class="pl-s"><span class="pl-pds">"</span>--transport<span class="pl-pds">"</span></span>, <span class="pl-s"><span class="pl-pds">"</span>stdio<span class="pl-pds">"</span></span>],
  <span class="pl-ent">"enabled"</span>: <span class="pl-c1">true</span>
}</pre></div>
</li>
</ol>
<p dir="auto">Comments in the rest of your file stay put. OpenCode auto-discovers<br>
the plugin file; no <code class="notranslate">plugin</code> array entry needed (adding one causes<br>
double-loading, see issue #10).</p>
<p dir="auto"><strong>Other tools</strong> — Claude Code, Cursor, Windsurf, Cline, Gemini CLI,<br>
and Codex use plain JSON configs without comment support, so the<br>
automated path is non-destructive there. Use <code class="notranslate">sqz init --only &lt;tool&gt;</code><br>
for those.</p>
<p dir="auto">That's it. Shell hooks installed, AI tool hooks configured.</p>
<h2 dir="auto">How It Works</h2>
<p align="center" dir="auto">
  <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" href="assets/sqz-architecture.png"><img src="assets/sqz-architecture.png" alt="sqz system architecture" width="700" style="max-width: 100%;"></a>
</p>
<p dir="auto">sqz installs a PreToolUse hook that intercepts bash commands before your AI tool runs them. The output gets compressed transparently — the AI tool never knows.</p>
<pre class="notranslate"><code class="notranslate">Claude → git status → [sqz hook rewrites] → compressed output (85% smaller)
</code></pre>
<p dir="auto">What gets compressed:</p>
<ul dir="auto">
<li><strong>Shell output</strong> — git, cargo, npm, docker, kubectl, ls, grep, etc.</li>
<li><strong>JSON</strong> — strips nulls, compact encoding</li>
<li><strong>Logs</strong> — collapses repeated lines</li>
<li><strong>Test output</strong> — shows failures only</li>
</ul>
<p dir="auto">What doesn't get compressed:</p>
<ul dir="auto">
<li>Stack traces, error messages, secrets — routed to safe mode (0% compression)</li>
<li>Your prompts and the AI's responses — controlled by the AI tool, not sqz</li>
</ul>
<h2 dir="auto">Supported Tools</h2>
<markdown-accessiblity-table><table role="table">
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Tool</th>

<th>Setup</th>
</tr>
</thead>

<td><code class="notranslate">sqz init</code></td>
</tr>

</tr>
<tr>

<td>PreToolUse hook (transparent)</td>
<td><code class="notranslate">sqz init</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cline</td>
<td>PreToolUse hook (transparent)</td>
<td><code class="notranslate">sqz init</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td><code class="notranslate">sqz init</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td>PreToolUse hook (transparent)</td>
<td><code class="notranslate">sqz init</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>

<h2 dir="auto">CLI</h2>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-shell" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate">sqz init --global             <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Install hooks for every project on this machine</span>
sqz init                      <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Install hooks for just this project</span>
sqz init --only kiro          <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Only configure Kiro (skip the rest)</span>
sqz init --only opencode      <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Only configure OpenCode (skip the rest)</span>
sqz init --skip cursor        <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Configure every agent except Cursor</span>
sqz compress <span class="pl-k">&lt;</span>text<span class="pl-k">&gt;</span>           <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Compress (or pipe from stdin)</span>
sqz compress --no-cache       <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Compress without dedup (always full output)</span>
sqz expand <span class="pl-k">&lt;</span>ref<span class="pl-k">&gt;</span>              <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Recover original content from a §ref:HASH§ token</span>
sqz compact                   <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Evict stale context to free tokens</span>
sqz gain                      <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Show daily token savings (bar chart)</span>
sqz gain --project <span class="pl-c1">.</span>          <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Per-project daily gains</span>
sqz gain --days 30            <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Last 30 days</span>
sqz stats                     <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Cumulative compression report</span>
sqz stats --breakdown         <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Per-command token usage breakdown</span>
sqz stats --project <span class="pl-c1">.</span>         <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Stats for current project only</span>
sqz stats --project list      <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> List all tracked projects</span>
sqz discover                  <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Find missed savings</span>
sqz resume                    <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Re-inject session context after compaction</span>
sqz vizit                     <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Live terminal dashboard (like htop for AI agents)</span>
sqz hook claude               <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Process a PreToolUse hook (Claude Code)</span>
sqz hook kiro                 <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Process a PreToolUse hook (Kiro)</span>
sqz print-opencode-plugin     <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Print OpenCode plugin TS for manual install</span>
sqz proxy --port 8080         <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> API proxy (compresses full request payloads)</span></pre></div>
<h3 dir="auto">Dedup Escape Hatch</h3>
<p dir="auto">When sqz sees the same content twice, it returns a compact <code class="notranslate">§ref:HASH§</code> token<br>
instead of the full text. Most models handle this fine, but some (e.g., GLM 5.1)<br>
can't parse the ref format and loop. Four ways to work around this:</p>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-shell" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate"><span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> 1. Recover original content from a ref</span>
sqz expand a1b2c3d4              <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> prefix match</span>
sqz expand <span class="pl-s"><span class="pl-pds">'</span>§ref:a1b2c3d4§<span class="pl-pds">'</span></span>     <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> paste the whole token</span>

<span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> 2. Compress without dedup (per-invocation)</span>
<span class="pl-c1">echo</span> <span class="pl-s"><span class="pl-pds">"</span>...<span class="pl-pds">"</span></span> <span class="pl-k">|</span> sqz compress --no-cache

<span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> 3. Disable dedup globally (env var)</span>
<span class="pl-k">export</span> SQZ_NO_DEDUP=1

<span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> 4. MCP passthrough tool (returns input byte-exact, zero transforms)</span>
<span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> Available via tools/list when sqz-mcp is running</span></pre></div>
<h2 dir="auto">Track Your Own Savings</h2>
<p dir="auto">Run <code class="notranslate">sqz gain</code> in your shell any time to see your own daily breakdown (see the<br>
Token Savings section above for what the output looks like), and <code class="notranslate">sqz stats</code><br>
for the full cumulative report:</p>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-shell" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate">$ sqz stats
  📊 sqz compression stats
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────

  178,442  tokens saved
  ↓  24.7% average reduction

  Compressions           3,003
  Tokens <span class="pl-k">in</span>              721,840
  Tokens out             543,398
  Tokens saved           178,442
  Avg reduction          24.7%

  🗄️  Cache
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Entries                43
  Size                   39.1 KB</pre></div>
<p dir="auto">Add <code class="notranslate">--breakdown</code> to see exactly which commands consume the most tokens:</p>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-shell" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate">$ sqz stats --breakdown

  🔍 Top Token Consumers
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  <span class="pl-c1">command</span>               calls  tokens <span class="pl-k">in</span>        out    saved
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  dedup                   249      45541       3237      93%
  stdin                    51      30851      24289      21%
  auto                    132      18288       7740      58%
  <span class="pl-c1">echo</span>                     17       1050        558      47%
  ls -la                    8        948        948       0%
  cargo build               7        170        145      15%
  git status                4         56          8      86%
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────</pre></div>
<p dir="auto"><strong>Per-project filtering:</strong></p>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-shell" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate">sqz stats --project <span class="pl-c1">.</span>           <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> stats for current project only</span>
sqz stats --project list        <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> list all tracked projects</span>
sqz gain --project <span class="pl-c1">.</span>            <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> daily gains for current project</span>
sqz gain --days 30              <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> last 30 days instead of 7</span>
sqz gain --days 30 --project <span class="pl-c1">.</span>  <span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> combine both</span></pre></div>
<p dir="auto">Stats are stored locally in SQLite under <code class="notranslate">~/.sqz/sessions.db</code> — nothing leaves your machine.</p>
<h2 dir="auto">How Compression Works</h2>
<ol dir="auto">
<li><strong>Per-command formatters</strong> — <code class="notranslate">git status</code> → compact summary, <code class="notranslate">cargo test</code> → failures only, <code class="notranslate">docker ps</code> → name/image/status table</li>
<li><strong>Structural summaries</strong> — code files compressed to imports + function signatures + call graph (~70% reduction). The model sees the architecture, not implementation noise.</li>
<li><strong>Dedup cache</strong> — SHA-256 content hash, persistent across sessions. Second read = 13-token reference.</li>
<li><strong>JSON pipeline</strong> — strip nulls → project out debug fields → flatten → collapse arrays → TOON encoding (lossless compact format)</li>
<li><strong>Safe mode</strong> — stack traces, secrets, migrations detected by entropy analysis and routed through with 0% compression</li>
</ol>
<p dir="auto">For the full technical details, see <a href="docs/">docs/</a>.</p>
<h2 dir="auto">Configuration</h2>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-toml" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate"><span class="pl-c"><span class="pl-c">#</span> ~/.sqz/presets/default.toml</span>
[<span class="pl-en">preset</span>]
<span class="pl-smi">name</span> = <span class="pl-s"><span class="pl-pds">"</span>default<span class="pl-pds">"</span></span>
<span class="pl-smi">version</span> = <span class="pl-s"><span class="pl-pds">"</span>1.0<span class="pl-pds">"</span></span>

[<span class="pl-en">compression</span>.<span class="pl-en">condense</span>]
<span class="pl-smi">enabled</span> = <span class="pl-c1">true</span>
<span class="pl-smi">max_repeated_lines</span> = <span class="pl-c1">3</span>

[<span class="pl-en">compression</span>.<span class="pl-en">strip_nulls</span>]
<span class="pl-smi">enabled</span> = <span class="pl-c1">true</span>

[<span class="pl-en">budget</span>]
<span class="pl-smi">warning_threshold</span> = <span class="pl-c1">0.70</span>
<span class="pl-smi">default_window_size</span> = <span class="pl-c1">200000</span></pre></div>
<h2 dir="auto">Privacy</h2>
<ul dir="auto">
<li>Zero telemetry — no data transmitted, no crash reports</li>
<li>Fully offline — works in air-gapped environments</li>
<li>All processing local</li>
</ul>
<h2 dir="auto">Development</h2>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-shell" dir="auto"><pre class="notranslate">git clone https://github.com/ojuschugh1/sqz.git
<span class="pl-c1">cd</span> sqz
cargo <span class="pl-c1">test</span> --workspace
cargo build --release</pre></div>

  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="&gt; Follow-up on my April 14 proposal — shipped a working version today as `v0.25.0` of DPC Messenger ([release](https://github.com/mikhashev/dpc-messenger/releases/tag/v0.25.0), [ADR-024](https://github.com/mikhashev/dpc-messenger/blob/main/docs/decisions/024-knowledge-graph-infrastructure.md)).
&gt;
&gt; The original mapping (Blob → fact, Tree → category, Commit → provenance, Branch → hypothesis) didn&#39;t survive contact with retrieval. Git&#39;s object model is fine for storage but graph queries over packfiles are prohibitive on every chat turn. We switched to SQLite with the same intent preserved at the schema layer: every edge carries a source taxonomy (`structural` / `gliner_ner` / `llm_relation`), a `needs_review` flag for LLM-extracted relations, and temporal `created_at` / `invalidated_at`. The &quot;branches as competing hypotheses&quot; idea became `needs_review` — uncertainty stays in the graph until resolved.
&gt;
&gt; One thing the original post missed: knowledge extraction is more of a *compilation pass* than a write. We run a sleep pipeline that reads session archives, extracts entities (GLiNER zero-shot NER) and relations (LLM with source-grounded prompts), then commits typed edges. The graph participates as a fourth retrieval channel alongside FAISS, BM25, and structural traversal. Actively dogfooded on this repo by one human and three agents (Ark in-process, CC via bridge, Iris on Discord). Responds to @a-a-k&#39;s &quot;no provenance, lossy compression&quot; critique from May 2 — provenance is in the edges, source taxonomy survives ingest, and the compilation step makes losses visible via `needs_review` flags rather than blending silently.
&gt;
&gt; Separate observation: Karpathy&#39;s HELLO.md gist (April 20) shows the same problem from the agent side — without continuity, an agent writes a goodbye letter. Our KG + sleep pipeline is a practical answer: agents build memory instead of goodbyes." data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">
      
                Copy Markdown

</clipboard-copy>  <div aria-live="polite" aria-atomic="true" class="sr-only" data-clipboard-copy-feedback></div>

  <h3 class="f5 text-normal py-2" style="flex: 1 1 auto">
    <div class="d-flex flex-items-center flex-wrap gap-1">

      </span>

      <strong>
            <a class="author Link--primary text-bold css-overflow-wrap-anywhere " show_full_name="false" data-hovercard-type="user" data-hovercard-url="/users/mikhashev/hovercard" data-octo-click="hovercard-link-click" data-octo-dimensions="link_type:self" href="/mikhashev">mikhashev</a>

      commented 


        <a href="/karpathy/442a6bf555914893e9891c11519de94f?permalink_comment_id=6148033#gistcomment-6148033" id="gistcomment-6148033-permalink" class="Link--secondary js-timestamp"><relative-time datetime="2026-05-13T11:32:50Z" class="no-wrap">May 13, 2026</relative-time></a>

        <div class="edit-comment-hide">
  <task-lists disabled sortable>
    <div class="comment-body markdown-body js-comment-body soft-wrap user-select-contain d-block">
      <blockquote>
<p dir="auto">Follow-up on my April 14 proposal — shipped a working version today as <code class="notranslate">v0.25.0</code> of DPC Messenger (<a href="https://github.com/mikhashev/dpc-messenger/releases/tag/v0.25.0">release</a>, <a href="https://github.com/mikhashev/dpc-messenger/blob/main/docs/decisions/024-knowledge-graph-infrastructure.md">ADR-024</a>).</p>
<p dir="auto">The original mapping (Blob → fact, Tree → category, Commit → provenance, Branch → hypothesis) didn't survive contact with retrieval. Git's object model is fine for storage but graph queries over packfiles are prohibitive on every chat turn. We switched to SQLite with the same intent preserved at the schema layer: every edge carries a source taxonomy (<code class="notranslate">structural</code> / <code class="notranslate">gliner_ner</code> / <code class="notranslate">llm_relation</code>), a <code class="notranslate">needs_review</code> flag for LLM-extracted relations, and temporal <code class="notranslate">created_at</code> / <code class="notranslate">invalidated_at</code>. The "branches as competing hypotheses" idea became <code class="notranslate">needs_review</code> — uncertainty stays in the graph until resolved.</p>
<p dir="auto">One thing the original post missed: knowledge extraction is more of a <em>compilation pass</em> than a write. We run a sleep pipeline that reads session archives, extracts entities (GLiNER zero-shot NER) and relations (LLM with source-grounded prompts), then commits typed edges. The graph participates as a fourth retrieval channel alongside FAISS, BM25, and structural traversal. Actively dogfooded on this repo by one human and three agents (Ark in-process, CC via bridge, Iris on Discord). Responds to <a class="user-mention notranslate" data-hovercard-type="user" data-hovercard-url="/users/a-a-k/hovercard" data-octo-click="hovercard-link-click" data-octo-dimensions="link_type:self" href="https://github.com/a-a-k">@a-a-k</a>'s "no provenance, lossy compression" critique from May 2 — provenance is in the edges, source taxonomy survives ingest, and the compilation step makes losses visible via <code class="notranslate">needs_review</code> flags rather than blending silently.</p>
<p dir="auto">Separate observation: Karpathy's HELLO.md gist (April 20) shows the same problem from the agent side — without continuity, an agent writes a goodbye letter. Our KG + sleep pipeline is a practical answer: agents build memory instead of goodbyes.</p>
</blockquote>
    </div>
  </task-lists>
  
</div>

<p dir="auto">Try ΩmegaWiki in Claude Code and run the full LLM-Wiki loop you proposed — ingest papers, build a typed knowledge graph, generate ideas, draft papers, respond to reviewers.</p>
<p dir="auto">End to end. One wiki. No chunks.</p>
<a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://private-user-images.githubusercontent.com/167013924/587468785-5ef50ec1-78cc-4a26-937b-16f50f81ce46.png?jwt=eyJ0eXAiOiJKV1QiLCJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiJ9.eyJpc3MiOiJnaXRodWIuY29tIiwiYXVkIjoicmF3LmdpdGh1YnVzZXJjb250ZW50LmNvbSIsImtleSI6ImtleTUiLCJleHAiOjE3Nzg3MTE1NzksIm5iZiI6MTc3ODcxMTI3OSwicGF0aCI6Ii8xNjcwMTM5MjQvNTg3NDY4Nzg1LTVlZjUwZWMxLTc4Y2MtNGEyNi05MzdiLTE2ZjUwZjgxY2U0Ni5wbmc_WC1BbXotQWxnb3JpdGhtPUFXUzQtSE1BQy1TSEEyNTYmWC1BbXotQ3JlZGVudGlhbD1BS0lBVkNPRFlMU0E1M1BRSzRaQSUyRjIwMjYwNTEzJTJGdXMtZWFzdC0xJTJGczMlMkZhd3M0X3JlcXVlc3QmWC1BbXotRGF0ZT0yMDI2MDUxM1QyMjI3NTlaJlgtQW16LUV4cGlyZXM9MzAwJlgtQW16LVNpZ25hdHVyZT0xNTkwYmRlN2Q2ZDVhMDExMWVhMDBjMWEyODcxZDhkN2NmNDg2MzRkOWM3NWJiZTFkMmVmZjIxMmNhZDcxNzRhJlgtQW16LVNpZ25lZEhlYWRlcnM9aG9zdCZyZXNwb25zZS1jb250ZW50LXR5cGU9aW1hZ2UlMkZwbmcifQ.lhx-f1dbc4E6Y3ssdRNX4V-RWtwUxO4Qep1T_IiAQM8"><img width="2000" height="1101" alt="微信图片_20260505122754_295_16" src="https://private-user-images.githubusercontent.com/167013924/587468785-5ef50ec1-78cc-4a26-937b-16f50f81ce46.png?jwt=eyJ0eXAiOiJKV1QiLCJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiJ9.eyJpc3MiOiJnaXRodWIuY29tIiwiYXVkIjoicmF3LmdpdGh1YnVzZXJjb250ZW50LmNvbSIsImtleSI6ImtleTUiLCJleHAiOjE3Nzg3MTE1NzksIm5iZiI6MTc3ODcxMTI3OSwicGF0aCI6Ii8xNjcwMTM5MjQvNTg3NDY4Nzg1LTVlZjUwZWMxLTc4Y2MtNGEyNi05MzdiLTE2ZjUwZjgxY2U0Ni5wbmc_WC1BbXotQWxnb3JpdGhtPUFXUzQtSE1BQy1TSEEyNTYmWC1BbXotQ3JlZGVudGlhbD1BS0lBVkNPRFlMU0E1M1BRSzRaQSUyRjIwMjYwNTEzJTJGdXMtZWFzdC0xJTJGczMlMkZhd3M0X3JlcXVlc3QmWC1BbXotRGF0ZT0yMDI2MDUxM1QyMjI3NTlaJlgtQW16LUV4cGlyZXM9MzAwJlgtQW16LVNpZ25hdHVyZT0xNTkwYmRlN2Q2ZDVhMDExMWVhMDBjMWEyODcxZDhkN2NmNDg2MzRkOWM3NWJiZTFkMmVmZjIxMmNhZDcxNzRhJlgtQW16LVNpZ25lZEhlYWRlcnM9aG9zdCZyZXNwb25zZS1jb250ZW50LXR5cGU9aW1hZ2UlMkZwbmcifQ.lhx-f1dbc4E6Y3ssdRNX4V-RWtwUxO4Qep1T_IiAQM8" style="max-width: 100%; height: auto; max-height: 1101px;; aspect-ratio: 2000 / 1101; background-color: var(--bgColor-muted); border-radius: 6px" class="js-gh-image-fallback"></a>

  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="**SwarmVault v3.14 — we made the onramp dead simple.** Realized we had a powerful tool with a steep first impression, so the last few releases focused on making the first 60 seconds effortless.

**`swarmvault quickstart &lt;input&gt;`** — the new beginner-friendly entry point. Give it a directory or a public GitHub URL and it does everything: init, ingest, compile, launch the viewer. One command, zero config.

```
npx @swarmvaultai/cli quickstart ./my-project
npx @swarmvaultai/cli quickstart https://github.com/user/repo
```

**`swarmvault next`** — a read-only orientation command that tells you exactly where you are and what to do next. Works in three states: uninitialized (&quot;run quickstart or init&quot;), initialized (&quot;add some sources&quot;), compiled (&quot;here&#39;s what you can do with your vault&quot;). JSON output for agents, human output for you.

**Simplified CLI help** — primary commands are front and center. Compatibility aliases and advanced graph commands are still there but hidden from the first screen so new users aren&#39;t overwhelmed.

The idea is: someone reads this gist, runs `npx @swarmvaultai/cli quickstart .`, and has a compiled wiki + knowledge graph + interactive viewer in under a minute. Then `swarmvault next` tells them what to explore from there.

Everything after that is progressive disclosure: `chat` for multi-turn conversations with your vault, `context build` for agent handoff packs, `export ai` for portable `llms.txt` bundles, `graph serve` for the visual workbench, `install --agent` for wiring into your coding tool.

Still local-first. Still works fully offline. Still MIT. 100+ releases and counting.

Repo: **https://github.com/swarmclawai/swarmvault**" data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">
      
                Copy Markdown

</clipboard-copy>  <div aria-live="polite" aria-atomic="true" class="sr-only" data-clipboard-copy-feedback></div>
</span>        </details-menu>
      </details>

      <strong>
            <a class="author Link--primary text-bold css-overflow-wrap-anywhere " show_full_name="false" data-hovercard-type="user" data-hovercard-url="/users/waydelyle/hovercard" data-octo-click="hovercard-link-click" data-octo-dimensions="link_type:self" href="/waydelyle">waydelyle</a>

      commented 


        <a href="/karpathy/442a6bf555914893e9891c11519de94f?permalink_comment_id=6148168#gistcomment-6148168" id="gistcomment-6148168-permalink" class="Link--secondary js-timestamp"><relative-time datetime="2026-05-13T13:31:12Z" class="no-wrap">May 13, 2026</relative-time></a>

        <div class="edit-comment-hide">
  <task-lists disabled sortable>
    <div class="comment-body markdown-body js-comment-body soft-wrap user-select-contain d-block">
      <p dir="auto"><strong>SwarmVault v3.14 — we made the onramp dead simple.</strong> Realized we had a powerful tool with a steep first impression, so the last few releases focused on making the first 60 seconds effortless.</p>
<p dir="auto"><strong><code class="notranslate">swarmvault quickstart &lt;input&gt;</code></strong> — the new beginner-friendly entry point. Give it a directory or a public GitHub URL and it does everything: init, ingest, compile, launch the viewer. One command, zero config.</p>
<pre class="notranslate"><code class="notranslate">npx @swarmvaultai/cli quickstart ./my-project
npx @swarmvaultai/cli quickstart https://github.com/user/repo
</code></pre>
<p dir="auto"><strong><code class="notranslate">swarmvault next</code></strong> — a read-only orientation command that tells you exactly where you are and what to do next. Works in three states: uninitialized ("run quickstart or init"), initialized ("add some sources"), compiled ("here's what you can do with your vault"). JSON output for agents, human output for you.</p>
<p dir="auto"><strong>Simplified CLI help</strong> — primary commands are front and center. Compatibility aliases and advanced graph commands are still there but hidden from the first screen so new users aren't overwhelmed.</p>
<p dir="auto">The idea is: someone reads this gist, runs <code class="notranslate">npx @swarmvaultai/cli quickstart .</code>, and has a compiled wiki + knowledge graph + interactive viewer in under a minute. Then <code class="notranslate">swarmvault next</code> tells them what to explore from there.</p>
<p dir="auto">Everything after that is progressive disclosure: <code class="notranslate">chat</code> for multi-turn conversations with your vault, <code class="notranslate">context build</code> for agent handoff packs, <code class="notranslate">export ai</code> for portable <code class="notranslate">llms.txt</code> bundles, <code class="notranslate">graph serve</code> for the visual workbench, <code class="notranslate">install --agent</code> for wiring into your coding tool.</p>
<p dir="auto">Still local-first. Still works fully offline. Still MIT. 100+ releases and counting.</p>
<p dir="auto">Repo: <strong><a href="https://github.com/swarmclawai/swarmvault">https://github.com/swarmclawai/swarmvault</a></strong></p>
    </div>
  </task-lists>
  
</div>

  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="# **Offline-first, SQLite-backed library** ready for production.

Repo and the superpowers documentation here: [[equationalapplications/expo-llm-wiki](https://github.com/equationalapplications/expo-llm-wiki)](https://github.com/equationalapplications/expo-llm-wiki)

Most RAG implementations treat every &quot;chunk&quot; of data as equal. The result? Your LLM gets &quot;context pollution&quot;—distracted by a random observation from three days ago while ignoring your core system instructions.

Inspired by Andrej Karpathy&#39;s LLM Wiki spec, the **`expo-llm-wiki`** monorepo introduces a **Tiered Memory Architecture**. This allows you to give your AI a structured &quot;brain&quot; using cross-entity namespaces and configurable weights.

---

## The &quot;Brain&quot; Hierarchy

The **LLM Librarian** manages a knowledge hierarchy that mimics human expertise:

1. **The Fact Tier (Immutable Truth)**
* **What it is:** Static documents (specs, PDFs).
* **The Role:** The highest source of truth; if the Librarian finds a contradiction, the **Fact** always wins.
* **The Benefit:** Immutable so hard truths never get diluted.


2. **The Working Memory Tier (The Context)**
* **What it is:** The active project environment (codebase or work-in-progress).
* **The Role:** Real-time episodic events and observations.
* **The Benefit:** Uses **recency weighting** to stay aligned with the &quot;now.&quot;


3. **The Wisdom Tier (The Evolving Wiki)**
* **What it is:** A synthesized repository where the Librarian &quot;remembers&quot; lessons and patterns.
* **The Role:** Consolidates Working Memory into long-term architectural or stylistic preferences.
* **The Benefit:** Uses **accessCount weighting** so frequently referenced &quot;lessons&quot; graduate into Core Wisdom.



---

## Production-Grade Superpowers

* **Hybrid Retrieval Engine:** Uses **Cosine Similarity** for semantic search when online, with an automatic fallback to **MiniSearch** for full-text search when the device is offline.
* **The Pipeline:** Uses `runLibrarian()` to consolidate episodic events into durable facts and `runHeal()` to resolve contradictions and prune stale claims.
* **Multi-Entity Architecture:** Support for thousands of isolated users/agents within a single SQLite database using `entityId` namespaces—zero memory leakage.
* **React &amp; Mobile Native Optimized:** Includes reactive hooks (`useMemoryRead`) and &quot;Emoji-Safe&quot; chunking to prevent common mobile LLM UI bugs.
* **Security &amp; GDPR:** Built-in `runPrune` and `forget` methods for &quot;Right to be Forgotten&quot; compliance, plus source normalization to prevent path injection.

---

## Implementation Example:

```typescript
const bundle = await wiki.read([&#39;facts&#39;, &#39;wip_codebase&#39;, &#39;wisdom_cache&#39;], &#39;Synthesize current state.&#39;);

const systemPrompt = formatContext(bundle, {
  factWeights: {
    confidence: 1.5,  // Prioritize immutable Facts
    recency: 0.9,     // Keep Working Memory relevant
    accessCount: 0.5  // Surface Wisdom that the user relies on most
  }
});

```

**Tech Stack:** Expo, React Native, SQLite, MiniSearch." data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">
